Charged scalar fields on Reissner–Nordström spacetimes II: late-time tails and instabilities
Dejan Gajic ∗ 1

Abstract
. This is the second part of a series of papers deriving the precise, late-time behaviour and (in)stability properties of charged scalar fields on near-extremal Reissner–Nordström spacetimes via energy estimates. In this paper, we use purely physical-space based methods to establish the precise late-time behaviour of solutions to the charged scalar field equation in the form of oscillating and decaying late-time tails that satisfy inverse-power laws, assuming global integrated energy decay estimates, which are proved in the companion paper [ Gaj26 ] . This paper provides the first pointwise decay estimates for charged scalar fields on black hole backgrounds without an assumption of smallness of the scalar field charge. We also prove the existence of asymptotic instabilities for the radiation field along future null infinity and, in the extremal case, also along the future event horizon. Both the energy methods and the precise late-time asymptotics derived in this paper are expected to play an important role in future nonlinear studies of black hole dynamics in the context of the spherically symmetric (Einstein–)Maxwell–charged scalar field equations, as well as in the context of extremal Kerr spacetimes.



1.
Introduction

Late-time dynamics around sub-extremal Kerr black holes solutions
[
Ker63
]
to the Einstein vacuum equations

{\rm Ric}[g]=0

(1.1)

are intimately related to the following three geometric properties of Kerr spacetimes
g=g_{M,a}
: the
red-shift
along null generators of the event horizon, the existence of
trapped null geodesics
and the presence of an
ergoregion
.

The latter two manifest themselves in the study of wave equations on Kerr as energy concentration around trapped null geodesics for finite times
[
Sbi15
]
and energy amplification in certain time-frequency regimes known as
rotational superradiance

[
Zel71
,
Zel72
]
. In contrast, red-shift emerges as a favourable energy decay mechanism near the event horizon
[
DR09
]
.

The study of late-time dynamics of waves on extremal Kerr black holes, members of the Kerr family that attain a maximal angular momentum
(|a|=M)
, becomes considerably more difficult, due to a
strong coupling
of superradiance with the
degeneration
of red-shift and the existence of trapped null geodesics arbitrarily close to the event horizon. The absence of red-shift and the coupling of geometric obstructions to decay may be thought of as the source of pointwise growth or asymptotic instabilities along the event horizon in the context of linear wave equations
[
Are15
,
CGZ16
,
GZ18
,
Gaj23a
]
.

A robust control of the strong coupling between the lack of red-shift, the trapping of null geodesics and superradiance for
linear
wave equations on fixed (near-)extremal Kerr backgrounds is fundamental for an understanding of the ultimate fates of dynamical spacetime solutions to (
1.1
) arising from small initial data perturbations of extremal Kerr data.

A shortcut towards shedding light on the nonlinear effects of the above strong coupling of difficulties in black hole dynamics, bypassing a full resolution in the extremal Kerr setting, is the consideration of small perturbations of (near-)extremal Reissner–Nordström initial data in the context of the spherically symmetric Einstein–Maxwell-(massless) charged scalar field (EMCSF) system of equations:

\displaystyle{\rm Ric}[g]-\frac{1}{2}R[g]g=

\displaystyle\>8\pi\left(\mathbb{T}^{EM}[g,F]+\mathbb{T}^{SF}[g,\phi]\right),

(1.2)

\displaystyle dF=

\displaystyle\>0,

(1.3)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\nu}F^{\nu\mu}=

\displaystyle\>4\pi\mathfrak{q}\textnormal{Im}\,(\phi\overline{\>(^{A}D)^{\mu}\phi}),

(1.4)

\displaystyle(g^{-1})^{\mu\nu}\>(^{A}D)_{\mu}\>(^{A}D)_{\nu}(\phi)=

\displaystyle\>0,

(1.5)

where
F
is a Faraday tensor, describing electromagnetic fields,
\phi
is a scalar field with associated charge parameter (or coupling constant)
\mathfrak{q}
,
\mathbb{T}^{EM}[g,F]
and
\mathbb{T}^{SF}[g,\phi]
are the electromagnetic and scalar field stress-energy tensors, respectively, and
\>{}^{A}D=\nabla-i\mathfrak{q}A\otimes(\cdot)
is the gauge derivative, with the 1-form
A
denoting an electromagnetic potential satisfying
dA=F
.

In the present paper, we perform a precise, quantitative analysis of the
linearization
of (
1.2
)–(
1.5
) around electrically charged (sub-)extremal Reissner–Nordström solutions:

\left(g_{M,Q}=-\Omega^{2}dt^{2}+\Omega^{-2}dr^{2}+r^{2}\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}},\quad F_{Q}=-Qr^{-2}dt\wedge dr,\quad\phi\equiv 0\right),

with
\Omega^{2}=1-2Mr^{-1}+Q^{2}r^{-2}
, which reduces to a single equation in spherical symmetry:

\displaystyle(g^{-1}_{M,Q})^{\mu\nu}\>(^{A}D)_{\mu}\>(^{A}D)_{\nu}\phi=0,

(1.6)

where
dA=F_{Q}
. Extremality corresponds to
|Q|=M
in this setting. The equation (
1.6
) is called the
charged scalar field equation
on a Reissner–Nordström background. Together with the companion paper
[
Gaj26
]
, the present paper forms the first mathematically rigorous, global analysis of (
1.6
) in both the sub-extremal and extremal cases without any restrictions on the size of the charge parameter
\mathfrak{q}
. This may be thought of as, in particular, a first step towards a global analysis of (
1.2
)–(
1.5
) for small perturbations of (near-)extremal Reissner–Nordström data. However, in contrast with the motivating problem (
1.2
)–(
1.5
), we will
not
assume spherical symmetry of
\phi
in our analysis of (
1.6
).

A global understanding of (
1.6
) relies on methods that can treat the degeneration of red-shift, the presence of trapped null geodesics as well as
charged superradiance

[
Bek73
,
Gib75
]
, which may be thought of as a close analogue of rotational superradiance.

In the first part of our series
[
Gaj26
]
, we establish global,
integrated energy estimates
via a combination of physical-space and Fourier-space based analysis in the setting of (near-)extremal Reissner–Nordström, which may be thought of as weak decay statements that serve as the foundation for the stronger, more precise decay statements derived in the present paper. The main results in
[
Gaj26
]
immediately extend to the full sub-extremal range of parameters under the additional assumption of mode stability on the real axis.

In the present paper, we obtain a complete understanding of precise, leading-order late-time behaviour and instability phenomena, starting from integrated energy estimates and using purely physical-space-based methods.

The main results of the present paper can be split into the following two theorems:

Theorem 1.1

(Late-time asymptotics; informal version of Theorem
4.1
)
.

Let
r^{-1}\psi
be a solution to (
1.6
) with
\mathfrak{q}\neq 0
with respect to the conformally smooth electromagnetic gauge
A=-\frac{Q}{r}d\tau
, with
\tau
a time function whose level sets are horizon-intersecting and asymptotically hyperboloidal spacelike hypersurfaces and with
\partial_{\tau}
a Killing vector field normalized at infinity that is timelike away from the event horizon. Assume that
\psi
arises from the evolution of smooth, compactly supported initial data. Then, for
1-\frac{|Q|}{M}\ll 1
or
|\mathfrak{q}Q|\ll 1
:

\displaystyle\psi(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)\sim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\\
\ell(\ell+1)<\mathfrak{q}^{2}Q^{2}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathbb{Z}\\
|m|\leq\ell\end{subarray}}\left[\Psi_{\ell m}^{\infty}(\tau,r;\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi])+\Psi^{+}_{\ell m}(\tau,r;\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{H}_{\ell m}[\psi])\right]Y_{\ell m}(\theta,\varphi)\quad(\tau\to\infty),

where
\Psi^{+}_{\ell m}(\tau,r;\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{H}_{\ell m}[\psi])=e^{-i\mathfrak{q}Qr_{+}^{-1}\tau}\Psi_{\ell m}^{\infty}\left(\tau,r_{+}+\frac{r_{+}^{2}}{r-r_{+}};-\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{H}_{\ell m}[\psi]\right)
, with
r_{+}
the event horizon area radius, and where
\Psi_{\ell m}^{\infty}
are globally-defined functions satisfying the following late-time asymptotics with
r_{0}\geq r_{+}
:

\displaystyle\Psi^{\infty}_{\ell m}(\tau,\infty;\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi])\sim

\displaystyle\>\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi]\tau^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}+i\mathfrak{q}Q},\quad

\displaystyle(\beta_{\ell}\in(0,1)),

(1.7)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\infty}_{\ell m}(\tau,\infty;\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi])\sim

\displaystyle\>\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi]\frac{\tau^{-\frac{1}{2}+i\mathfrak{q}Q}}{\log\tau},\quad

\displaystyle(\beta_{\ell}=0),

(1.8)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\infty}_{\ell m}(\tau,\infty;\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi])\sim

\displaystyle\>\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi]\tau^{-\frac{1}{2}-\sigma\frac{\beta_{\ell}}{2}+i\mathfrak{q}Q}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\zeta^{\sigma n}\tau^{-\sigma n\beta_{\ell}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+i\mathfrak{q}Q-(n+\frac{1}{2})\sigma\beta_{\ell}\right)},\quad

\displaystyle(\beta_{\ell}\in i(0,\infty)),

(1.9)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\infty}_{\ell m}(\tau,r_{0};\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi])\sim

\displaystyle\>\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi]\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}(r_{0};\mathfrak{q}Q)\tau^{-1-\beta_{\ell}},\quad

\displaystyle(\beta_{\ell}\in(0,1)),

(1.10)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\infty}_{\ell m}(\tau,r_{0};\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi])\sim

\displaystyle\>\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi]\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}(r_{0};\mathfrak{q}Q)\frac{\tau^{-1}}{\log^{2}\tau},\quad

\displaystyle(\beta_{\ell}=0),

(1.11)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\infty}_{\ell m}(\tau,r_{0};\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi])\sim

\displaystyle\>\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi]\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}(r_{0};\mathfrak{q}Q)\tau^{-1-\sigma\beta_{\ell}}

(1.12)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\zeta^{\sigma n}\frac{\tau^{-\sigma n\beta_{\ell}}}{\Gamma(\sigma\beta_{\ell}+1)\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma\beta_{\ell}+i\mathfrak{q}Q)\Gamma(-(n+1)\sigma\beta_{\ell})},

\displaystyle(\beta_{\ell}\in i(0,\infty)).

Here,

•

\beta_{\ell}:=\sqrt{(2\ell+1)^{2}-4\mathfrak{q}^{2}Q^{2}}\in[0,1)\cup i(0,\infty)
,

•

\sigma=\,\textnormal{sign}\,(\mathfrak{q}Q)
,
\zeta=\zeta(\mathfrak{q}Q,\beta_{\ell})
is a complex constant (defined in Theorem
4.1
) and
\Gamma(\cdot)
is the gamma function,

•

\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi]
and
\mathfrak{h}_{\ell m}[\psi]
are linear functionals of (derivatives of) initial data at
\tau=0
, so that each
\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi]
vanishes only for a codimension-
1
set of initial data,
\mathfrak{H}_{\ell m}[\psi]\equiv 0
when
|Q|<M
and in the case
|Q|=M
, each
\mathfrak{H}_{\ell m}[\psi]
also vanishes only for a codimension-
1
set of initial data,

•

\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}Y_{\ell m}
are time-independent solutions to (
1.6
) that are smooth at
r=r_{+}
and satisfy
\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}(r_{+};\mathfrak{q}Q)=1
.

In the case that
1-\frac{|Q|}{M}\gtrsim 1
and
|\mathfrak{q}Q|\gtrsim 1
, the above results remain valid under the condition of the validity of mode stability on the real axis.

Theorem 1.2

(Instabilities and energy concentration; informal version of Theorem
4.3
)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.1
, the following asymptotic instability and non-decay results hold:

(i)

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there exist constants
\mathfrak{c}_{k}>0
, such that for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
\ell(\ell+1)<\mathfrak{q}^{2}Q^{2}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
with
|m|\leq\ell
:

\displaystyle\limsup_{\tau\to\infty}(1+\tau)^{-k-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{\ell}}{2}}|D_{X}^{k+1}\psi_{\ell m}|(\tau,r_{+})\geq

\displaystyle\>|\mathfrak{c}_{k}||\mathfrak{H}_{\ell m}[\psi]|\quad

\displaystyle(|Q|=M,\>\beta_{\ell}\neq 0),

(1.13)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\tau\to\infty}\log(1+\tau)(1+\tau)^{-k-\frac{1}{2}}|D_{X}^{k+1}\psi_{\ell m}|(\tau,r_{+})\geq

\displaystyle\>|\mathfrak{c}_{k}||\mathfrak{H}_{\ell m}[\psi]|\quad

\displaystyle(|Q|=M,\>\beta_{\ell}=0),

(1.14)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\tau\to\infty}(1+\tau)^{-k-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{\ell}}{2}}|(r^{2}D_{X})^{k+1}\psi_{\ell m}|(\tau,\infty)\geq

\displaystyle\>|\mathfrak{c}_{k}||\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi]|\quad

\displaystyle(\beta_{\ell}\neq 0),

(1.15)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\tau\to\infty}\log(1+\tau)(1+\tau)^{-k-\frac{1}{2}}|(r^{2}D_{X})^{k+1}\psi_{\ell m}|(\tau,\infty)\geq

\displaystyle\>|\mathfrak{c}_{k}||\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi]|\quad

\displaystyle(\beta_{\ell}=0),

(1.16)

with
X
a radial vector field tangential to
\Sigma_{\tau}
, the level sets of
\tau
.

(ii)

When
|\mathfrak{q}Q|>\frac{1}{2}
(
\beta_{0}\in i(0,\infty)
),
energy concentration
occurs near
\mathcal{H}^{+}
in the case
|Q|=M
and, with additional
r
-weights, also near
\mathcal{I}^{+}
when
|Q|\leq M
, in the following sense: there exists a constant
\mathfrak{c}_{\rm int}>0
, such that for all
r_{+}\leq r_{H}<r_{I}<\infty
:

\displaystyle\limsup_{\tau\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq r_{H}\}}\mathbb{T}^{SF}[g_{M,Q},r^{-1}\psi](\mathbf{n}_{\tau},\mathbf{n}_{\tau})\,d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\geq

\displaystyle\>\mathfrak{c}_{\rm int}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\\
\ell+\frac{1}{2}<|\mathfrak{q}Q|\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathbb{Z}\\
|m|\leq\ell\end{subarray}}|\mathfrak{H}_{\ell m}[\psi]|^{2},

(1.17)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r>r_{H}\}}\mathbb{T}^{SF}[g_{M,Q},r^{-1}\psi](\mathbf{n}_{\tau},\mathbf{n}_{\tau})\,d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}=

\displaystyle\>0,

(1.18)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\tau\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq r_{I}\}}r^{2}|D_{r}\psi|^{2}\,\sin\theta d\theta d\varphi dr\geq

\displaystyle\>\mathfrak{c}_{\rm int}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\\
\ell+\frac{1}{2}<|\mathfrak{q}Q|\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathbb{Z}\\
|m|\leq\ell\end{subarray}}|\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi]|^{2},

(1.19)

with induced volume form
d\mu_{\tau}
on
\Sigma_{\tau}
and
\mathbf{n}_{\tau}
the corresponding future-directed normal.

In the case that
1-\frac{|Q|}{M}\gtrsim 1
and
|\mathfrak{q}Q|\gtrsim 1
, the above results remain valid under the condition of the validity of mode stability on the real axis.

Precise and more general versions of the above theorems are stated later in the paper in the form of Theorems
4.1
and
4.3
. In the next section we provide some initial remarks.

1.1.
Initial remarks

In this section, we place Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
in a broader context, discuss some new dynamical aspects of charged scalar fields that follow from the theorems, and we mention some remaining open problems. We will also discuss some related previous work. An overview of further, related previous work can be found in §
1.2
.

1.1.1.
Neutral scalar fields

In the case
\mathfrak{q}=0
, (
1.6
) reduces to the wave equation on Reissner–Nordström:

\square_{g_{M,Q}}\phi=0.

(1.20)

While there is no superradiance present in this setting, the absence of red-shift alone already leads to the existence of an asymptotic instability in the extremal case, known as the
Aretakis instability

[
Are11
]
, which can be stated as follows: for
k\geq 1
, there exists a constant
c_{k}>0
such that

||\partial_{r}^{k+1}\psi(\tau,r_{+},\cdot)||_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\geq c_{k}H_{0}[\psi]\tau^{k},

(1.21)

with
H_{0}[\psi]=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\partial_{r}(r\psi)|_{\Sigma_{0}\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}}\,\sin\theta d\theta d\varphi
. Observe that the
\tau
-growth in (
1.21
) is
slower
than the
\tau
-growth in (
1.13
) and (
1.14
). Furthermore, whereas
H_{0}[\psi]
vanishes for initial data supported away from the event horizon and the instabilities become weaker (by losing a power of
\tau
growth
[
Are13
]
), we will show that the constants
\mathfrak{h}_{\ell m}[\psi]
are generically
non-vanishing
, even for data supported away from the horizon.

A precise quantitative analysis of (
1.20
) was performed in
[
AAG20a
]
, where an analogue of Theorem
1.1
was obtained. There, it was also shown that the non-degenerate energy decays in
\tau
, in contrast with (
1.17
).

Another important difference with the present paper is that the instability (
4.3
) follows from the existence of conservation laws along the event horizon and does not rely on a quantitative understanding of the asymptotics derived in
[
AAG20a
]
. On the other hand, Theorem
1.1
is crucial for proving Theorem
1.2
. The inability to rely on conservation laws in a derivation of instabilities is a key motivation for deriving precise late-time asymptotics in the present paper.

In view of the above comparisons, the instabilities in Theorem
1.2
may be thought of as being considerably
stronger
than the Aretakis instability.

In the neutral scalar field setting, there are related results in the context of the full nonlinear EMCSF system (
1.2
)–(
1.5
) with
\mathfrak{q}=0
, in which
Q
is not a dynamical quantity. See the recent
[
AKU26b
]
for a complete stability statement in the form of an analysis of the moduli space of initial data leading to Reissner–Nordström spacetimes with different masses, providing, in particular, a mathematical confirmation of earlier numerical work in
[
MRT13
]
. We also refer to the earlier codimension-1 stability result of extremal Reissner–Nordström in
[
AKU26a
]
and the related nonlinear analysis in the neutral scalar field setting (outside of spherical symmetry) in
[
Ang16
,
AAG20b
,
AU25
]
.

1.1.2.
Comparison with the extremal Kerr setting

The instabilities along the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
(
r=r_{+}
) in the
|Q|=M
case in Theorem
1.2
bear a close resemblance to the
azimuthal instabilities
along the extremal Kerr (
|a|=M
) event horizon in
[
Gaj23a
]
[Theorem 4.2], if we restrict to
\beta_{\ell}\in i(0,\infty)
and replace
\beta_{\ell}
with:

\sqrt{1+4\Lambda_{m\ell}(a\upomega_{+})-8m^{2}},

where
\Lambda_{m\ell}(a\upomega_{+})
are Boyer–Lindquist oblate spheroidal eigenvalues at the superradiant treshold frequency
m\upomega_{+}
, labelled by the azimuthal number
m\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, with
\ell\geq|m|
. These instabilities were first discovered via a heuristic Fourier-based analysis around
\omega=m\upomega_{+}
in
[
CGZ16
]
and then
[
GZ18
]
.

One may think of the aziumuthal number
m
as playing the role of the dimensionless charge parameter
\mathfrak{q}Q
, see also
[
Gaj26
]
[§1.3] for a closer comparison in Fourier space.

In contrast with Theorem
1.2
,
[
Gaj23a
]
[Theorem 4.2] relies on a global integrability assumption. Failure of this integrability assumption would correspond to the (stronger) instability scenarios (b) and (c) in
[
Gaj23a
]
[Corollary 4.3]. Furthermore, the present paper also treats the cases
\beta_{\ell}\in[0,1)
.

The improvements in the present paper when compared to
[
Gaj23a
]
are closely related to the application of uniform estimates that apply in both the near-extremal and extremal settings and are derived in
[
Gaj26
]
. The new methods introduced in the present paper remain applicable in the setting in
[
Gaj23a
]
and will be applied to improve the results in
[
Gaj23a
]
in future work.

Similarly, the late-time tails in Theorem
1.1
in the
|Q|=M
case, away from future null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{+}
are analogues of the late-time tails derived in
[
Gaj23a
]
[Theorem 4.1]. However, while
[
Gaj23a
]
[Theorem 4.1] is conditional on a priori qualitative global integrability assumptions, Theorem
1.1
is entirely unconditional when
0\leq 1-\frac{|Q|}{M}\ll 1
.

The late-time tails along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, on the other hand, are particular to the setting of CSF and differ from those derived in
[
Gaj23a
]
[Theorem 4.1].

1.1.3.
A conformal isometry

The symmetry between the behaviour along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
\mathcal{I}^{+}
in the
|Q|=M
case is related to the existence of the Couch–Torrence conformal isometry
[
CT84
]

\Phi_{\rm C-T}
. Initial data for
\psi
imposed on a hypersurface that is invariant under
\Phi_{\rm C-T}
, such that
\psi\circ\Phi_{\rm C-T}=\psi
will lead to identical behaviour along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
and
\mathcal{H}^{+}
up to additional change in sign of
\mathfrak{q}
; see Proposition
2.2
.

One may interpret the asymptotic instabilities of
r^{2}
-weighted derivative quantities in (
1.15
) and (
1.16
) as close analogues of horizon instabilities. In fact, the asymptotic instabilities along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
are also present in the sub-extremal setting. This is not surprising, since
\mathcal{I}^{+}
can be viewed as a Killing horizon with zero surface gravity after performing an appropriate conformal rescaling of the metric.

1.1.4.
Wagging tails and null infinity signatures of extremality

In contrast with the uncharged scalar field setting, the late-time asymptotics in Theorem
1.1
feature oscillations in
\tau
at different levels.

First of all, note that
e^{-i\mathfrak{q}f}\psi
is a solution to (
1.6
) with respect to the electromagnetic potential
A+df
for any smooth function
f
, so an overall phase factor, by itself, has no gauge-invariant meaning and can be absorbed via an appropriate (time-dependent) redefinition of
A
. The presence or absence of the phase factor
e^{-i\mathfrak{q}Q\tau}
does play an important, gauge-invariant role when we consider the gauge-invariant time derivatives
|D_{\tau}\psi|
, see §
1.1.5
for a further discussion.

In the
\beta_{\ell}\in i(0,\infty)
case however, the gauge-invariant quantity
|\psi|
also features oscillations in its late-time asymptotics, since the asymptotics are governed by an infinite sum of oscillating terms, see (
1.9
) and (
1.12
).

1.1.5.
The role of Minkowski and Bertotti–Robinson

The projection of
\Psi^{\infty}
to spherical harmonics with angular frequency
\ell
has the following structure (see Theorem
4.1
):

\Psi^{\infty}_{\ell}=\frac{\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}}{\mathfrak{w}^{0}_{\ell}}(\Psi_{0}^{\infty})_{\ell},

where

(m^{-1})^{\mu\nu}\>(^{\widehat{A}}D)_{\mu}\>(^{\widehat{A}}D)_{\nu}(r^{-1}\Psi^{\infty}_{0})=0,

(1.22)

with
m
the Minkowski metric and
\widehat{A}=-Qr^{-1}du
(with respect to Bondi coordinates
(u,r)
) and where:

\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}^{0}=\begin{cases}\alpha_{+}r^{\frac{1}{2}+iq-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}}+\alpha_{-}r^{\frac{1}{2}+iq-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}}\quad&(\beta_{\ell}\neq 0),\\
\alpha_{+}r^{\frac{1}{2}+iq}\log r+\alpha_{-}r^{\frac{1}{2}+iq}\quad&(\beta_{\ell}=0),\end{cases}

with
\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}^{0}Y_{\ell m}
time-independent solutions to (
1.22
) and
\alpha_{\pm}\in\mathbb{C}
the constants appearing in the large-
r
asymptotics of
\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}
:

\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}=\begin{cases}\alpha_{+}r^{\frac{1}{2}+iq-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}}(1+O(r^{-1})+\alpha_{-}r^{\frac{1}{2}+iq-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}}(1+O(r^{-1})\quad&(\beta_{\ell}\neq 0),\\
\alpha_{+}r^{\frac{1}{2}+iq}\log r(1+O(r^{-1})+\alpha_{-}r^{\frac{1}{2}+iq}(1+O(r^{-1})\quad&(\beta_{\ell}=0).\end{cases}

Similarly, the function
\Psi^{+}_{0}(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi):=\Psi^{\infty}_{0}(\tau,r_{+}+\frac{r_{+}^{2}}{r-r_{+}},\theta,\varphi)
is a solution to:

(g_{\rm B-R}^{-1})^{\mu\nu}\>(^{A_{0}}D)_{\mu}\>(^{A_{0}}D)_{\nu}(r_{+}^{-1}\Psi^{+}_{0})=0,

(1.23)

with

\left(g_{\rm B-R}=r_{+}^{2}(-x^{2}d\tilde{v}^{2}-2d\tilde{v}dx+\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}),\quad F=-r_{+}d\tilde{v}\wedge dx\right)

the Bertotti–Robinson solution (or near-horizon extremal Reissner–Nordström solution)
[
Ber59
,
Rob59
]
to the Einstein–Maxwell equations and
A_{0}=-r_{+}\tilde{x}dv
.
1
1

1

Note that the conformally rescaled Minkowski spacetime
(\mathbb{R}^{1+3},r^{-2}m)
can be embedded in a Bertotti–Robinson spacetime; see
[
GVdM24
]
[§1.5.1].

The leading-order late-time asymptotics of solutions to (
1.6
) are therefore determined by appropriate solutions to the charged scalar field equation on Minkowski and Bertotti–Robinson (up to the factors
\frac{\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}}{\mathfrak{w}^{0}_{\ell}}
)!

Note, however, that while the Bertotti–Robinson solutions determine the asymptotic behaviour of
|\psi|
along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, they do
not
determine the asymptotic behaviour of the tangential gauge derivative
|D_{\tau}\psi|
along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
:
2
2

2

When
\beta_{\ell}\in i(0,\infty)
the asymptotics include an infinite sum of oscillating factors, which we omit here for ease of notation.

|D_{\tau}\psi_{\ell m}|(\tau,r_{+})\sim|\mathfrak{q}Q||\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi]|\begin{cases}\tau^{-1-\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{\ell}}\quad&(\beta_{\ell}\in(0,1)\cup i(0,\infty)),\\
\frac{\tau^{-1}}{\log^{2}\tau}\quad&(\beta_{\ell}=0).\end{cases}

This illustrates how one must proceed with caution when translating late-time properties of waves on near-horizon geometries to the behaviour along the event horizon of genuine extremal black hole spacetimes.

In particular, the leading-order late-time behaviour of
D_{\tau}\psi_{\ell m}
at
r=r_{+}
therefore agrees in the extremal and sub-extremal cases (in stark contrast with the
\mathfrak{q}=0
setting!). This implies in particular that in
both
the extremal and sub-extremal cases the stress-energy tensor satisfies:

\mathbb{T}^{SF}[g_{M,Q},r^{-1}\psi_{\ell m}]|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}(\partial_{\tau},\partial_{\tau})\sim\mathfrak{q}^{2}Q^{2}|\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi]|^{2}\begin{cases}\tau^{-2-2\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{\ell}}\quad&(\beta_{\ell}\in(0,1)\cup i(0,\infty)),\\
\frac{\tau^{-2}}{\log^{4}\tau}\quad&(\beta_{\ell}=0).\end{cases}

In other words, the energy density of
\psi
measured with respect to the Killing vector field
\partial_{\tau}
,
tangential
to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is dictated by late-time tails arising from far-away behaviour of the wave operator, at
null infinity
, and it decays in time.

On the other hand, we have by (
1.13
) and (
1.14
) that the energy density
\mathbb{T}^{SF}[g_{M,Q},\psi_{\ell m}]|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}(\partial_{r},\partial_{r})

transversal
to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is dictated by tails arising from the
event horizon
, described by
\Psi_{0}^{+}
, and features growth in time.

By analogy (recall §
1.1.3
), we also obtain along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
in the extremal case:

|D_{T}\psi_{\ell m}|(\tau,\infty)\sim|\mathfrak{q}Q||\mathfrak{H}_{\ell m}[\psi]|\tau^{-1-\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{\ell}}.

Gauge-invariant time derivatives along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
therefore decay faster in the sub-extremal case than in the extremal case and in the latter, we can read off the value of the constants
|\mathfrak{H}_{\ell m}[\psi]|
from the limiting behaviour of
|D_{\tau}\psi_{\ell m}|
at
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, thereby providing an “null infinity signature” of extremality and the constants
\mathfrak{H}_{\ell m}[\psi]
responsible for the extremal instability phenomena in Theorem
1.2
; see
[
AAG18a
,
AKS25
]
for results on null infinity signatures in the neutral scalar field setting.

1.1.6.
Transient instabilities along near-extremal event horizons

In the estimates that are implicit in the tilde-notation in Theorem
1.1
, we keep precise track of the dependence on the surface gravity
\kappa_{+}
of the background Reissner–Nordström spacetime; see also the precise statements in Theorem
4.1
. This allows us to estimate the time interval in the near-extremal setting for which the instabilities of Theorem
1.2
are relevant.

We show that for
\delta_{1}\ll\delta_{2}\ll 1
and
\kappa_{+}\leq\delta_{1}r_{+}^{-1}
:

\displaystyle\sup_{\delta_{1}\kappa_{+}^{-1}\leq\tau\leq\delta_{2}\kappa_{+}^{-1}}(\tau+1)^{-k-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{0}}|D_{X}^{k+1}\psi_{\ell m}|(\tau,r_{+})\geq

\displaystyle\>\frac{|\mathfrak{c}_{k}|}{2}\left|\mathfrak{H}_{\ell m}[\psi_{M}]\right|\quad

\displaystyle(\beta_{0}\neq 0),

\displaystyle\sup_{\delta_{1}\kappa_{+}^{-1}\leq\tau\leq\delta_{2}\kappa_{+}^{-1}}\log(1+\tau)\tau^{-k-\frac{1}{2}}|D_{X}^{k+1}\psi_{\ell m}|(\tau,r_{+})\geq

\displaystyle\>\frac{|\mathfrak{c}_{k}|}{2}\left|\mathfrak{H}_{\ell m}[\psi_{M}]\right|\quad

\displaystyle(\beta_{0}=0).

where
\psi_{M}
is the solution to (
1.6
) arising from the same initial data as
\psi
, but corresponding to the metric
g_{M,Q}
with
|Q|=M
instead of
|Q|<M
; see Proposition
11.1
.

Therefore,
|D_{X}^{k+1}\psi_{\ell m}|
must attain a maximum along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
greater or equal to a constant that scales like
\kappa_{+}^{-\frac{1}{2}-k+\frac{1}{2}\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{0}}
in the case
|Q|<M
when
\beta_{0}\neq 0
(and similarly with an additional decaying factor
(\log\kappa_{+}^{-1})^{-1}
when
\beta_{0}=0
). This may be thought of as a
transient instability
. See also the results in
[
GZZ16
]
that are consistent with the above observations.

1.1.7.
Sub-extremality and mode (in)stability

The results in Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
apply also away from extremality under the additional assumption of mode stability on the real axis; see Condition
1
for the precise assumption. Roughly, this corresponds to the absence of oscillating, finite-energy solutions with an exponential time dependence.

Mode stability in the charged scalar field context remains an open problem away from extremality. See also
[
Gaj26
]
[Appendix B] for a partial mode stability result and
[
Gaj26
]
[Remark 1.4] for a further discussion.

In the extremal case, and by continuity, in the near-extremal case, mode stability is shown to hold in
[
Gaj26
]
, which allows for a proof of the desired integrated energy estimates in these settings in
[
Gaj26
]
. This stands in sharp contrast with the presence of growth and instabilities along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
in Theorem
1.2
, which can be associated with the behaviour in Fourier space at frequencies
\omega=\mathfrak{q}Qr_{+}^{-1}
, which are actually
excluded
in the mode stability statements as modes are ill-defined at those frequencies in the extremal case.

Note that for sufficiently small
|\mathfrak{q}Q|
, mode stability follows straightforwardly from mode stability of the neutral scalar field; see also
[
Gaj26
]
[§4].

We also note that in the coupled Maxwell–charged scalar field system of equations (
1.3
)–(
1.5
), where

g=g_{M,Q}
, the sum of stress energy tensors
\mathbb{T}^{EM}[g,F]+\mathbb{T}^{SF}[g,\phi]
satisfies a conservation law and corresponding integrals along appropriate horizon-intersecting asymptotically hyperboloidal or null hypersurfaces are non-negative definite and non-increasing, implying in particular that
|\phi|
must be bounded in time and small for initially small perturbations of the Reissner–Nordström Faraday tensor.

Boundedness of
|\phi|
in the nonlinear setting alone, however, does not imply the absence of exponentially growing modes in the linearized setting, as it is possible that
|\phi|
does not decay (sufficiently fast) and hence, that the linearized equations do not approximate the nonlinear equations at late-times. We note that for small
|\mathfrak{q}Q|
, decay of
|\phi|
has been proved in
[
VdM22
]
and the numerical results in
[
HP98c
,
BR16
,
ÁVV25
]
are consistent with decay of
|\phi|
for large
|\mathfrak{q}Q|
.

In view of the above, a proof of mode (in)stability away from extremality in the context of (
1.6
) remains an interesting open problem for understanding (
1.2
)–(
1.5
) within the context of small perturbations of sub-extremal Reissner–Nordström initial data with large
|\mathfrak{q}Q|
,
away from extremality
.

1.1.8.
Late-time tails for
\psi_{\geq\ell}

Theorem
1.1
determines the leading-order late-time asymptotics for
\psi
supported on spherical harmonics with angular parameter greater or equal to
\ell
,
\psi_{\geq\ell}
, provided
\ell(\ell+1)<\mathfrak{q}^{2}Q^{2}
. However, the methods in the present paper also extend straightforwardly to
\ell(\ell+1)\geq\mathfrak{q}^{2}Q^{2}
. The key difference is the necessity to consider higher-order time integrals
(TK)^{-k}
,
k\geq 2
; see the discussion in §
1.3
. We omit this analysis in the present paper.

1.2.
Further previous literature

In this section, we will provide references to literature pertaining to late-time tails and precise late-time asymptotics in the Reissner–Nordström setting. We refer to
[
Gaj26
]
[§1.1] for further references pertaining to integrated energy estimates.

1.2.1.
Numerics and heuristics

A study of late-time tails for (
1.6
) on sub-extremal Reissner–Nordström black holes was instigated by
[
HP98b
]
, see also the earlier work restricted to small
|\mathfrak{q}Q|
in
[
HP98a
]
. In these works a heuristic analysis was performed in Fourier space, restricted to a neighbourhood of
\omega=0
. When
\beta_{\ell}\in(0,1)
, the tails in
[
HP98b
]
are consistent with Theorem
1.1
. When
\beta_{\ell}\in i(0,\infty)
, the present paper elucidates in addition the precise oscillatory structure in the tails suggested in
[
HP98b
]
, which are precisely captured by the tail function
\Psi^{\infty}
.

Late-time tails in the extremal setting were first investigated along the event horizon (corresponding to initial data supported away from the horizon) in the setting of (
1.6
) in
[
Zim17
]
, via a mixed heuristic-numerical analysis in performed in Fourier space around the frequency
\omega=\mathfrak{q}Q
. The instabilities suggested by
[
Zim17
]
are consistent with Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
. The present work also covers the case
\beta_{\ell}=0
and provides also, in particular, a precise characterization of the oscillatory, late-time asymptotics along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
in the form of the tail function
\Psi^{+}
. Furthermore, as the Fourier analysis
[
Zim17
]
does not consider frequencies near
\omega=0
, their dominant role in the late-time tails of the derivatives
|D_{\tau}\psi|
(captured by the tail function
\Psi^{\infty})
was not observed; see §
1.1.4
for a further discussion.

Late-time tails in the setting of the nonlinear EMCSF equations in spherical symmetry (
1.2
)–(
1.5
), have also been studied numerically in the sub-extremal setting, starting from
[
HP98c
]
and more recently in
[
BR16
,
ÁVV25
]
reporting results consistent with the analysis of the linearized system in the present paper when
\beta_{\ell}\neq 0
.

In the extremal setting, nonlinear EMCSF equations were first studied numerically in
[
GP26
]
. See also
[
GP25
]
for earlier numerical work on the Maxwell–charged scalar field equations (EMCSF with
F=F_{Q}
). The late-time behaviour in the context of spacetimes settling down to extremal Reissner–Nordström that is observed numerically is consistent with the linear asymptotics derived in the present paper.

1.2.2.
Late-time tails for neutral scalar fields

Late-time tails in the context of the wave equation on black hole spacetimes (neutral scalar fields) have a rich history, starting from heuristic work of Price
[
Pri72
]
. The decay rates corresponding to sub-extremal black hole backgrounds are referred to as “Price’s law”. On sub-extremal Reissner–Nordström spacetimes, the existence and nature of late-time tails was first proved in
[
AAG18c
,
AAG18b
]
. In
[
Hin22
,
AAG23a
]
, late-time tails were proved to exist also on sub-extremal Kerr spacetimes. Late-time tails for scalar fields supported on higher spherical harmonics consistent with Price’s law were established on Reissner–Nordström in
[
Hin22
,
AAG23b
]
, see also
[
AAG23a
]
for a discussion on higher spherical harmonics in Kerr. We refer to
[
AAG23a
]
for further discussion on the late-time tails literature in the black hole setting. Note that the rates in late-time tails change if one considers
dynamical
instead of stationary spacetimes or adds nonlinearities to the wave equation. Furthermore, the rates change when considering initial data that are motivated by gravitational
N
-body problems with no incoming radiation. We point the reader to
[
GK22
,
LO24
,
GK25
]
and references therein.

Let us note here that the late-time tails in Theorem
1.1
are less sensitive to the fall-off in
r
of the initial data when compared to the neutral setting.

Late-time tails on extremal black holes were first established in
[
AAG20a
]
on extremal Reissner–Nordström and then in
[
Gaj23a
]
on extremal Kerr. See §
1.1.1
for a further discussion on this setting.

1.2.3.
Charged scalar fields

The mathematical literature on late-time time tails and precise late-time asymptotics in the context of charged scalar fields is more sparse. In contrast with neutral scalar fields, late-time tails are already present for charged scalar fields on Minkowski
3
3

3

Note that the linearization of the Maxwell–Klein Gordon equations around the Minkowski solution results in the standard (neutral) wave equation. One can nevertheless consider the gauge derivative
D=d-i\mathfrak{q}QA\otimes(\cdot)
corresponding to the Reissner–Nordström electric charge
Q
and consider the associated charged scalar field equation, which we refer to as the charged scalar field equation on Minkowski.
by
[
GVdM24
]
, see also the related sharp decay results in the massless Dirac–Coulomb system established in
[
BGRM25
]
.

In the black hole setting, decay estimates for solutions to the spherically symmetric Maxwell–Klein Gordon equations on sub-extremal Reissner–Nordström were established in the small-
|\mathfrak{q}Q|
setting in
[
VdM22
]
; see also §
1.1.7
.

Let us also note that the behaviour of solutions to the spherically symmetric EMCSF system in dynamically sub-extremal black hole interiors has been studied,
assuming
late-time decay along the event horizon that is consistent with Theorem
1.1
. In
[
VdM18
]
,
C^{0}
-stability and
C^{2}
-instability of the Cauchy horizon was established for
|\mathfrak{q}Q_{f}|<\frac{1}{2}
, with
Q_{f}
the charge of the Reissner–Nordström black hole that the corresponding dynamical black holes solution settles down to in the black hole exterior. The
|\mathfrak{q}Q_{f}|<\frac{1}{2}
condition is motivated by expectation that in that case
|\phi|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}
decays integrably along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
in that case, which is consistent with Theorem
1.1
.

In
[
KVdM24
]
, the
|\mathfrak{q}Q_{f}|\geq\frac{1}{2}
case was considered and
C^{0}
-stability of the Cauchy horizon was established, relying on the oscillation present in
\psi|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}
with respect to the gauge
A=-Q(r^{-1}-r_{+}^{-1})d\tau
, which follows directly from Theorem
1.1
(after changing the gauge
A=-Qr^{-1}d\tau
to
A=-Q(r^{-1}-r_{+}^{-1})d\tau
).

In
[
GL19
]
, the extremal setting, the behaviour for solutions to the spherically symmetric EMCSF system were studied in the interiors of dynamical black holes settling down to extremal Reissner–Nordström, with a smallness condition on
|\mathfrak{q}Q|
. It was shown that Cauchy horizons are stable in
C^{0}\cap H^{1}
and are sufficiently regular so that the spacetime can be extended as a solution to the EMCSF system across the horizons. The initial data assumptions for the energy decay along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
in
[
GL19
]
are consistent with the results in the present paper; see also the discussion in §
1.1.5
.

Finally, we note that some of the difficulties in establishing late-time tails in the charged scalar field setting, assuming suitable integrated energy estimates, are already present in the setting of neutral scalar fields with additional asymptotically inverse-square potentials. See
[
Gaj23b
,
Hin23
]
for proofs on the existence of late-time tails and references therein for previous work on decay estimates in this setting. In particular, the philosophy of proving late-time tails by subtracting appropriate global tail functions and integrating in time, introduced in
[
Gaj23b
]
, remains applicable to the setting of the present paper.

1.3.
Sketch of the proofs of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2

We will consider energy densities of functions
\widehat{\psi}
that are defined as follows:

\widehat{\psi}=\psi-\Psi

where
r^{-1}\psi
is a solution to (
1.6
) with respect to the conformally smooth electromagnetic gauge
A=-\frac{Q}{r}d\tau
and
\Psi
is the
tail function
from Theorem
1.1
, which, as we will sketch here, approximates
\psi
as
\tau\to\infty
.

The coefficients
\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi]
and
\mathfrak{h}_{\ell m}[\psi]
are complex numbers that can be explicitly determined from initial data for
\psi
at
\tau=0
and we will illustrate how they are determined in Step 2 below.

For convenience, we introduce the dimensionless charge parameter:

q=\mathfrak{q}Q.

The relevant energy densities take the following form:

\mathcal{E}_{p}[\widehat{\psi}]\sim(\Omega^{-1}r)^{p}\Omega^{2}|\partial_{r}\widehat{\psi}|^{2}+(\Omega^{-1}r)^{\min\{p,0\}}r^{-2}\left[|\partial_{\tau}\widehat{\psi}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\widehat{\psi}|^{2}+|\widehat{\psi}|^{2}\right],

where
p\in\mathbb{R}
,
\Omega^{2}=1-\frac{2M}{r}+\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}}
and
\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
is the covariant derivative with respect to the unit round sphere.

Note that in the case
p=2
and for bounded
r
,
\mathcal{E}_{p}[\widehat{\psi}]\sim\mathbb{T}(\mathbf{n}_{\tau},\mathbf{n}_{\tau})
, so
\mathcal{E}_{2}[\widehat{\psi}]
controls a non-degenerate energy density. Furthermore, away from
\mathcal{H}^{+}
,
\mathcal{E}_{p}[\widehat{\psi}]
may be viewed as an
r^{p}
-weighted energy density, as first introduced in
[
DR10
]
.

We write:

(g^{-1}_{M,Q})^{\mu\nu}\>(^{A}D)_{\mu}\>(^{A}D)_{\nu}(r^{-1}\widehat{\psi})=-(g^{-1}_{M,Q})^{\mu\nu}\>(^{A}D)_{\mu}\>(^{A}D)_{\nu}(r^{-1}\Psi)=:G_{A}.

1.3.1.
Step 0: integrated energy estimates

The starting point of the present paper is the following uniform integrated energy estimate, established in
[
Gaj26
]
. Let
1<p<\max\{1+\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{0},2\}+\epsilon
, with
\epsilon>0
arbitrarily small, then for all
\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}
:

\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{2}}}\mathcal{E}_{p-2\epsilon}[\widehat{\psi}]\,d\sigma dr+\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\upzeta(r)\cdot\mathcal{E}_{p-1-2\epsilon}[\widehat{\psi}]+r^{-2}|\widehat{\psi}|^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau\leq C\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{1}}}\mathcal{E}_{p}[\widehat{\psi}]\,d\sigma dr\\
+C\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}(\ldots)|r^{3}G_{A}|^{2}+(1-\upzeta)|\partial_{\tau}G_{A}|^{2}\,d\sigma dr,

(1.24)

where
\upzeta
is a function supported away from a small neighbourhood of the photon sphere at
r=r_{\sharp}
and
(\ldots)
denotes appropriate weights that we will not state in the present sketch; see however Theorem
2.5
for a detailed version.

When applied to
\psi
, with
r^{-1}\psi
a solution to (
1.6
), (
1.24
) implies the following energy boundedness and weak energy decay estimates:

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{2}}}\mathcal{E}_{p-2\epsilon}[\widehat{\psi}]\,d\sigma dr\leq

\displaystyle\>C\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{1}}}\mathcal{E}_{p}[\widehat{\psi}]\,d\sigma dr,

(1.25)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{k}}}\upzeta\cdot\mathcal{E}_{p-1-2\epsilon}[\widehat{\psi}]\,d\sigma dr\to

\displaystyle\>0\quad\textnormal{for some monotonically increasing sequence $\{\tau_{k}\}$},

(1.26)

where the latter follows from the mean-value theorem and the factor
\upzeta
can be removed after additional commutation with
\partial_{\tau}
or angular derivatives. Note in particular the loss in
\epsilon
in the uniform energy estimate! This loss can be removed with an additional smallness assumption on the charge parameter
q
:
|q|<\frac{1}{4}
.

In the present paper, we will improve the decay estimate (
1.26
), but we will not do so directly at the level of
\widehat{\psi}
. Instead, we first consider
(TK)^{k}(\widehat{\psi})
, where
T=\partial_{\tau}
and

K=T+iqr_{+}^{-1}\mathbf{1}.

The underlying idea is that
(TK)^{k}\widehat{\psi}
should decay faster for each application of
TK
. We illustrate this as follows: along
r=r_{0}
, we expect (ignoring coefficients and oscillations and possible
\log\tau
factors) by Theorem
1.1
:

\displaystyle\psi_{\ell=0}(\tau,r_{0},\cdot)\sim c_{r_{0}}\tau^{-s}\quad(|Q|>M),

\displaystyle\psi_{\ell=0}(\tau,r_{0},\cdot)\sim\tau^{-s}+e^{-iqr_{+}^{-1}\tau}\tau^{-s}\quad(|Q|=M),

for appropriate values of
s
. In the sub-extremal case,
T^{k}\psi
should therefore decay with a rate
\tau^{-s-k}
, whereas in the extremal case we need to apply
(TK)^{k}
to obtain
\tau^{-s-k}
, due to the presence of the oscillating factor
e^{-i\mathfrak{q}Qr_{+}^{-1}\tau}
. Since we are considering the extremal and sub-extremal cases simultaneously, we therefore simply consider
(TK)^{k}
in both cases.

1.3.2.
Step 1: energy decay in time for
TK
-derivatives

We will discuss here the case
\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{0}=0
, which is the most difficult case, as then
p
in (
1.24
) satisfies
1<p<1+\epsilon
, so that (
1.24
) provides effectively a single weighted integrated estimate by taking, for example,
p=1+\frac{\epsilon}{2}
.

It is straightforward to show that (
1.24
) also holds when
\widehat{\psi}
is replaced with
\mathbf{Z}^{\gamma}\widehat{\psi}
, which schematically denotes:

\mathbf{Z}^{\gamma}\widehat{\psi}=\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{\gamma_{1}}((r-r_{+})\partial_{r})^{\gamma_{2}}\partial_{\tau}^{\gamma_{2}}\widehat{\psi},\quad|\gamma|=k.

Then we observe that

\mathcal{E}_{p}[(TK)\widehat{\psi}]\lesssim\sum_{|\gamma|\leq 1}\mathcal{E}_{p-2}[\mathbf{Z}^{k}\widehat{\psi}]+\mathcal{E}_{p-2}[\mathbf{Z}^{k}T\widehat{\psi}]+(\Omega^{-1}r)^{2-p}(r^{-2}|r^{3}G_{A}|^{2}+r^{-2}|r^{3}TG_{A}|^{2}).

(1.27)

Assuming sufficient decay on the inhomogeneity
G_{A}
(which is derived in §
7.2
), we can therefore extend the range of
p
from
p\in\{1+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\}
to
p\in\{1+\frac{\epsilon}{2},3+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\}
by considering
(TK)\widehat{\psi}
instead of
\widehat{\psi}
.

We will establish (assuming again sufficient decay of
G_{A}
): for
1<p<2
,

\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\mathcal{E}_{p}[(TK)\widehat{\psi}]\,d\sigma drd\tau\lesssim\tau^{-3+p+\nu},

(1.28)

with
\nu>0
arbitrarily small (for
\epsilon
sufficiently small). This step involves an interpolation argument, where we partition the
r
-interval as follows:

\{(r^{-2}(r-r_{+})\leq(1+\tau)^{-\sigma}\}\cup\{(r^{-2}(r-r_{+})>(1+\tau)^{-\sigma}\}.

We start with
\sigma=0
and apply (
1.27
) to obtain:

\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\mathcal{E}_{1+\epsilon}[(TK)\widehat{\psi}]\,d\sigma drd\tau\lesssim\tau^{-1+\nu}.

Then we take
\sigma=\frac{1}{2}
and use the already established energy decay estimate to obtain an improvement. We consider iteratively
\sigma=1-2^{-k}
and take
k
sufficiently large to obtain the desired energy decay estimate. One may view these estimates as a replacement of the energy decay estimates introduced in
[
DR10
]
, applicable with only limited available
r^{p}
-weighted energy estimates. See the proof of Proposition
6.1
for details.

In this step it is important to note that even though the argument involves a large number of iterations, the total number of derivatives on the right-hand side of the estimate is bounded and
independent
of the number of iterations.

The improved decay for
(TK)^{k}\widehat{\psi}
then follows analogously and requires the consideration of
\mathbf{Z}^{k}\widehat{\psi}
and
\mathbf{Z}^{k}T\widehat{\psi}
.

Observe that the above argument resembles the method for deriving energy decay estimates in
[
Gaj23b
]
and
[
Gaj23a
]
, with the key differences being:

•

The consideration of a non-trivial inhomogeneity, in contrast with
[
Gaj23b
]
,

•

Commutation with
TK
instead of
T
, in contrast with
[
Gaj23a
]
.

1.3.3.
Step 2: constructing time integral initial data

Note that Step 1 only produces energy decay estimates for
(TK)^{k}\widehat{\psi}
with
k\geq 1
, whereas we need suitably strong energy decay estimate for
\widehat{\psi}
itself.

The main technique here is the operation of the “time inversion” operator
(TK)^{-1}
, which we can interpret as
time integration
in the following way:

(TK)^{-1}(f)=-\frac{r_{+}}{iq}\left(K^{-1}f-T^{-1}f\right),

(1.29)

with

\displaystyle(T^{-1}f)(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)=

\displaystyle-\int_{\tau}^{\infty}f(\tau^{\prime},r,\theta,\varphi)\,d\tau^{\prime},

\displaystyle(K^{-1}f)(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)=

\displaystyle-e^{-iqr_{+}^{-1}\tau}\int_{\tau}^{\infty}e^{iqr_{+}^{-1}\tau^{\prime}}f(\tau^{\prime},r,\theta,\varphi)\,d\tau^{\prime}.

In view of the fact that
\psi
does not decay integrably in time when
\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{0}=0
(see Theorem
1.1
) we cannot hope to define
(TK)^{-1}(\psi)
. For this reason, we consider
(TK)^{-1}(\widehat{\psi})
, which, is well-defined since, as we will show,
\widehat{\psi}
does decay integrably in
\tau
along constant
r
hypersurfaces (away from
r=r_{+}
).

However,
a priori
, we do not have any pointwise decay for
\widehat{\psi}
, so we can only interpret the above time integrals
formally
. That is to say, we will construct functions
(TK)^{-1}(\widehat{\psi})
that only
a foriori
can be shown to satisfy the above integral identities.

Instead, we define
T^{-1}\widehat{\psi}
and
K^{-1}\widehat{\psi}
as solutions to the equations:

\displaystyle(g^{-1}_{M,Q})^{\mu\nu}\>(^{A}D)_{\mu}\>(^{A}D)_{\nu}(r^{-1}T^{-1}\widehat{\psi})=

\displaystyle\>T^{-1}G_{A},

\displaystyle(g^{-1}_{M,Q})^{\mu\nu}\>(^{A}D)_{\mu}\>(^{A}D)_{\nu}(r^{-1}K^{-1}\widehat{\psi})=

\displaystyle\>K^{-1}G_{A},

arising from initial data
(T^{-1}\widehat{\psi},T(T^{-1}\widehat{\psi}))|_{\Sigma_{0}}
and
(K^{-1}\widehat{\psi},T(K^{-1}\widehat{\psi}))|_{\Sigma_{0}}
, respectively, such that

\displaystyle T(T^{-1}\widehat{\psi})|_{\Sigma_{0}}=

\displaystyle\>\widehat{\psi}|_{\Sigma_{0}},

\displaystyle K(K^{-1}\widehat{\psi})|_{\Sigma_{0}}=

\displaystyle\>\widehat{\psi}|_{\Sigma_{0}}.

The above conditions determine equations for
T^{-1}\widehat{\psi}|_{\Sigma_{0}}
and
K^{-1}\widehat{\psi}|_{\Sigma_{0}}
of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(T^{-1}\widehat{\psi}|_{\Sigma_{0}})=r\widetilde{G}_{A},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{L}}(K^{-1}\widehat{\psi}|_{\Sigma_{0}})=r\underline{\widetilde{G}}_{A},

where
\mathcal{L}
is a second-order differential operator that contains only
\partial_{r},\partial_{\theta},\partial_{\varphi}
derivatives and the
\partial_{\tau}
-derivatives, which can be expressed in terms of
\widehat{\psi}|_{\Sigma_{0}}
and
T\widehat{\psi}|_{\Sigma_{0}}
, form part of
r\widetilde{G}_{A}
.

For this procedure to make sense, we need
G_{A}
to decay suitably fast in
\tau
, so that (
1.29
) with
f=G_{A}
is well-defined.

In particular, the operator
\mathcal{L}
takes the following form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}f=\partial_{r}(\Omega^{2}\partial_{r}f)+r^{-2}\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}f-\frac{d\Omega^{2}}{dr}r^{-1}f+2iq(1+O(r^{-2}))r^{-1}\partial_{r}f+\left[iqr^{-2}+O(r^{-2})\right]f.

In the case
\mathfrak{q}Q=0
, the differential operators
\mathcal{L}
and
\underline{\mathcal{L}}
may be thought of a degenerate elliptic operators and
r
-weighted, degenerate elliptic estimates apply. When
\mathfrak{q}Q\neq 0
, the principal symbol of
\mathcal{L}
remains unchanged, but the coercivity of the corresponding (degenerate) energies is destroyed.

Restricting to high angular frequencies
\psi|_{\ell\geq L}
with
L\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
suitably large and integrating by parts
r^{-s}(\Omega^{-1}r)^{-p^{\prime}}\partial_{r}(r^{-1}T^{-1}\psi)\mathcal{L}(T^{-1}\psi)
we maintain coercivity and obtain
r
-weighted elliptic-type estimates, see §
8.2
for the details. This does not, however, work for bounded angular frequencies.

We therefore do not construct
T^{-1}\psi
(and similarly,
K^{-1}\psi
) by inverting
\mathcal{L}
. Instead, we first construct the spherical harmonic modes
(T^{-1}\psi)_{\ell}
by considering an appropriate
twisted operator

\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}_{\ell}
and use the elliptic-type estimates valid at high angular frequencies to be able to sum the spherical harmonic modes in
\ell
.

The twisted operator
\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}_{\ell}
is defined as follows:

\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}_{\ell}(f):=\partial_{r}(\Omega^{2}\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}^{2}e^{-2iqr_{+}^{-1}\int_{r_{0}}^{r}r^{\prime-1}\Omega^{-2}(r^{\prime})(1-\mathbbm{h}(r^{\prime}))\,dr^{\prime}}\partial_{r}(\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}^{-1}f)),

so that

\widecheck{\mathcal{L}}_{\ell}((T^{-1}\psi)_{\ell}|_{\Sigma_{0}})=\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}e^{-2iqr_{+}^{-1}\int_{r_{0}}^{r}r^{\prime-1}\Omega^{-2}(r^{\prime})(1-\mathbbm{h}(r^{\prime}))\,dr^{\prime}}r(\widetilde{G}_{A})_{\ell}.

Here,
\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}
are the functions appearing in Theorem
1.1
. By considering
\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}^{-1}T^{-1}\widehat{\psi}_{\ell}
, we therefore arrive at an ODE which can simply be solved by integrating in the
r
-direction.

We start integrating at
r=r_{+}
and assume the corresponding boundary term at
r=r_{+}
vanishes, which is our first boundary condition. The second boundary condition is that
\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}^{-1}T^{-1}\psi_{\ell}|_{\Sigma_{0}}
vanishes as
r\to\infty
.

When
\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{\ell}<1
, we need the following condition for the above boundary conditions to be compatible:

\int_{r_{+}}^{\infty}\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}e^{-2i\mathfrak{q}Qr_{+}^{-1}\int_{r_{0}}^{r}r^{\prime-1}\Omega^{-2}(r^{\prime})(1-\mathbbm{h}(r^{\prime}))\,dr^{\prime}}r(\widetilde{G}_{A})_{\ell}\,dr=0.

(1.30)

The requirement (
1.30
) fixes the constants
\mathfrak{I}_{m\ell}[\psi]
appearing in the definition of
\Psi^{\infty}
!

An analogous procedure for
K^{-1}\psi
, fixes the constants
\mathfrak{h}_{m\ell}[\psi]
and hence the entire function
\Psi
, which determines the late time asymptotics. We refer to §
8.1
for the details of the above argument.

Finally, to obtain
T^{-1}\widehat{\psi}
and
K^{-1}\widehat{\psi}
, we need to sum
(T^{-1}\widehat{\psi})_{\ell}
and
(K^{-1}\widehat{\psi})_{\ell}
in
\ell
. The convergence of this sum follows from the above mentioned elliptic estimates that are valid for large
\ell
; see §
8.2
.

We conclude in particular finiteness of the relevant energy norms of
(TK)^{-1}\widehat{\psi}
appearing on the right-hand side of the energy decay estimates in Step 1.

1.3.4.
Step 3: energy decay and late-time asymptotics

In Step 2, we constructed
(TK)^{-1}\psi|_{\Sigma_{0}}
and obtained finiteness of weighted energies along
\Sigma_{0}
. Applying (
1.28
) to
(TK)^{-1}\widehat{\psi}
, we then obtain for
1<p<2
:

\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\mathcal{E}_{p}[\widehat{\psi}]\,d\sigma drd\tau\lesssim\tau^{p-3+\nu}.

(1.31)

See §
9
for the details.

By a standard argument, (
1.31
) results in the pointwise decay estimate:

|\psi-\Psi|=|\widehat{\psi}|\lesssim\tau^{-1+\nu},

which already implies the late-time asymptotics along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
stated in Theorem
1.1
, and, in the case
|Q|=M
, also along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, because
\Psi
decays slower than
\tau^{-1+\nu}
. See §
10
for the details.

We can increase the range of
p
in (
1.31
) to
-\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{0}<p<3
, by applying once more the elliptic estimates from Step 2. This then results in an additional pointwise estimate which degenerates at
r=r_{+}
and
r=\infty
:

|\psi-\Psi|=|\widehat{\psi}|\lesssim(r\Omega)^{-1+\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{0}}\tau^{-\frac{3}{2}-\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{0}+\nu},

from which we can conclude late-time asymptotics away from
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

To conclude precise late-time asymptotics in the sub-extremal case
|Q|<M
along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, we apply in addition standard red-shift energy estimates that remove the degeneracy at
r=r_{+}
in the above estimates, at the expense of introducing
\kappa_{+}
-dependence in the constants.

1.3.5.
Step 4: instabilities

The existence of pointwise instabilities along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
\mathcal{I}^{+}
may be thought of as a consequence of the precise late-time asymptotics for
\psi
that follow from Step 3. In the sketch below, we will therefore write:

\psi=\Psi+\ldots,

and we will ignore the terms in
+\ldots
, as they will not contribute to leading-order in the growth estimates below. It therefore remains to investigate the behaviour of
r^{2}X\Psi
and of
\mathcal{E}_{2}[\Psi]
to conclude the instabilities in Theorem
1.2
. In view of the symmetry of
\Psi
under the conformal Couch–Torrence isometry, it suffices to consider
r^{2}\Psi|_{\mathcal{I}^{+}}
.

When
\mathfrak{I}_{\ell m}[\psi]\neq 0
or
\mathfrak{H}_{\ell m}[\psi]\neq 0
, it easily follows from the expressions of
\Psi
in terms of
\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}
and
\Psi_{0}
and the asymptotics of
\Psi_{0}
that in the case
\beta_{\ell}\in[0,1)
:

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq R\}}\mathcal{E}_{2}[\Psi]\,d\sigma dr\sim

\displaystyle\>(1+\tau)^{-\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{\ell}}\quad(\beta_{\ell}\neq 0),

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq R\}}\mathcal{E}_{2}[\Psi]\,d\sigma dr\sim

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{\log^{2}(2+\tau)}\quad(\beta_{\ell}=0).

The leading-order behaviour for
r^{2}X\Psi|_{\mathcal{I}^{+}}
similarly follows in a straightforward manner when
\beta_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}
.

The energy and pointwise growth are more subtle when
\beta_{\ell}\in i(0,\infty)
, since
r^{2}X\Psi
then features oscillating terms that can in principle cancel at certain values of
\tau
.

The details of the derivation of instabilities can be found in §
11
.

1.4.
Overview of the remainder of the paper

We outline the structure of the remaining sections of the paper:

•

In §
2
, we provide the geometric preliminaries and notation that is relevant for the subsequent analysis. We introduce the relevant coordinate chart and the main equations expressed in this coordinate chart. This section moreover contains the global integrated estimates that we will assume in the present paper and which are derived in the companion paper
[
Gaj26
]
; see §
2.7
.

•

We introduce the notion of
stationary solutions
in §
3
, as well as a detailed derivation of the relevant properties of the functions
\mathfrak{w}_{\ell}
that appeared in the preceding sections.

•

In §
4
, we state precise versions of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
, making use of the notation introduced in §§
2
–
3
.

•

In §
5
, we derive additional, higher-order commuted integrated energy estimates by applying
(\Omega^{-1}r)^{p}
-weighted energy estimates near
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

•

We derive time-decay for weighted energies for
TK
-derivatives of solutions to (
1.6
) in §
6
.
These are the main decay estimates of the paper!

•

In §
7
, we construct the functions
\Psi
, which determine the leading late-time behaviour of
\psi
. We show that
(g^{-1}_{M,Q})^{\mu\nu}({}^{A}D)_{\mu}({}^{A}D)_{\nu}(r^{-1}\Psi)
decays suitably fast in
r
and
\tau
.

•

We then subtract
\psi-\Psi
and construct time integrals
(TK)^{-1}(\psi-\Psi)
in §
8
. We additionally derive elliptic-type estimates in this section.

•

In §
9
, we apply the energy decay estimates of §
6
to the time integrals constructed in §
8
to obtain energy decay for the difference
\psi-\Psi
.

•

In §
10
, we conclude the leading-order pointwise behaviour of
\psi
(late-time tails) by converting the energy decay estimates of §
9
into pointwise decay estimates for the difference
\psi-\Psi
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

•

We derive growth and instability in §
11
and prove Theorem
1.2
.

•

In Appendix
A
, we construct solutions to (
1.22
) in Minkowski,
\Psi_{0}^{\infty}
that determine the late-time asymptotics in Reissner–Nordström. This section is crucial for establishing the decay properties of
(g^{-1}_{M,Q})^{\mu\nu}({}^{A}D)_{\mu}({}^{A}D)_{\nu}(r^{-1}\Psi)
and determining the late-time behaviour of
\Psi
in different regions in spacetime.

1.5.
Acknowledgments

We thank Mihalis Dafermos, Christoph Kehle, Ryan Unger and Peter Zimmerman for interesting and helpful discussions relating to the setting of the present paper. The author acknowledges funding through the ERC Starting Grant 101115568.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we will define the relevant geometric concepts and notation, and we introduce the charged scalar field equation. We will also state the global integrated energy decay and energy boundedness estimates that follow from
[
Gaj26
]
.

2.1.
Reissner–Nordström

The
Reissner–Nordström black hole exteriors
are spacetimes
(\mathcal{M}_{M,Q},g_{M,Q})
, with
M>0
,
Q\in\mathbb{R}
,
|Q|\leq M
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{M,Q}=

\displaystyle\>\mathbb{R}_{v}\times[r_{+},\infty)_{r}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}_{(\theta,\varphi)},\quad\textnormal{with $\mathbb{S}^{2}$ the round unit sphere},

\displaystyle g_{M,Q}=

\displaystyle-\Omega^{2}dv^{2}+2dvdr+r^{2}\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}},\quad\textnormal{where}

\displaystyle\Omega^{2}(r)=

\displaystyle\>1-\frac{2M}{r}+\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}}=r^{-2}(r-r_{+})(r-r_{-}),\quad r_{-}\leq r_{+},

\displaystyle\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}:=

\displaystyle\>d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi^{2},

and the global causal vector field
T=\partial_{v}
is future-directed.
4
4

4

The coordinates
(\theta,\varphi)
cover
\lx@sectionsign^{2}
away from a half great circle connecting the north and south poles.

The
future event horizon

\mathcal{H}^{+}
is defined as the boundary:

\mathcal{H}^{+}:=\partial\mathcal{M}_{M,Q}=\{r=r_{+}\}.

The corresponding inverse or dual metric is:

g_{M,Q}^{-1}=\Omega^{2}\partial_{r}\otimes\partial_{r}+\partial_{v}\otimes\partial_{r}+\partial_{r}\otimes\partial_{v}+r^{-2}\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{-1},

with
\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{-1}
the inverse metric associated to the unit round sphere metric
\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
.

The
surface gravity

\kappa_{+}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\kappa_{+}:=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d\Omega^{2}}{dr}(r_{+})=\frac{Mr_{+}-Q^{2}}{r_{+}^{3}}.

When
|Q|=M
, we have that
r_{+}=M
and
\kappa_{+}=0
. In that case, we say the Reissner–Nordström black hole exterior is
extremal
. If
|Q|>M
, we have that
r_{+}>M
and
\kappa_{+}>0
, and we say the Reissner–Nordström black hole exterior is
sub-extremal
. We will refer to sub-extremal spacetimes as
near-extremal
, when the dimensionless quantity
r_{+}\kappa_{+}
is assumed to be small but non-zero.

In order to compare more easily the analysis near
r=r_{+}
and for
r\geq R\gg r_{+}
, it will be convenient to introduce the following alternative radial coordinates:

\displaystyle\rho_{+}:=

\displaystyle\>r_{+}^{-1}-r^{-1},

\displaystyle\rho_{\infty}:=

\displaystyle\>r^{-1}.

Note that
\rho_{+},\rho_{\infty}\in[0,r_{+}^{-1})
. We state the following lemma from
[
Gaj26
]
:

Lemma 2.1

(Lemma 2.2 from
[
Gaj26
]
)
.

Applying standard “Big-O notation” (see for example
[
Gaj26
]
[§2.2]), we can estimate:

\displaystyle r^{-2}\Omega^{2}(r(\rho_{+}))=

\displaystyle\>2\kappa_{+}\rho_{+}+(1-6\kappa_{+}r_{+})\rho_{+}^{2}+(-2r_{+}+6\kappa_{+}r_{+}^{2})\rho_{+}^{3}+r_{+}^{2}\left(1-2\kappa_{+}r_{+}\right)\rho_{+}^{4},

\displaystyle r^{-2}\Omega^{2}(r(\rho_{\infty}))=

\displaystyle\>\rho_{\infty}^{2}-2(r_{+}-\kappa_{+}r_{+}^{2})\rho_{\infty}^{3}+r_{+}^{2}\left(1-2\kappa_{+}r_{+}\right)\rho_{\infty}^{4}.

The
photon sphere radius
is defined as follows:

r_{\sharp}:=\frac{3M}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{9M^{2}-8Q^{2}},

and it is the solution to the equaton
\frac{d}{dr}(r^{-2}\Omega^{2})(r)=0
in
(r_{+},\infty)
.

We define the
tortoise coordinate

r_{*}:(r_{+},\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
as the solution to the ODE:

\displaystyle\frac{dr_{*}}{dr}=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{\Omega^{2}},

\displaystyle r_{*}(r_{\sharp})=

\displaystyle\>0.

2.2.
Foliations

We introduce the following smooth
foliation-defining functions
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}:[r_{+},\infty)\to[0,\infty),\quad

\displaystyle 2-\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}\Omega^{2}\geq 0,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{h}:[r_{+},\infty)\to[0,\infty),\quad

\displaystyle\mathbbm{h}(r):=2-\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}\Omega^{2}(r).

Then we define the time function
\tau:\mathcal{M}_{M,Q}\to\mathbb{R}
as follows:

\tau(v,r,\theta,\varphi):=v-\int_{r_{+}}^{r}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}(r^{\prime})\,dr^{\prime}.

Denote
\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}}:=\{\tau=\tau^{\prime}\}\subset\mathcal{M}_{M,Q}
. Then
\mathcal{M}_{Q,M}=\bigcup_{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}}\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}}
. Note that the level sets
\Sigma_{\tau}
intersect
\mathcal{H}^{+}
at
v=\tau
.

The tuple
(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)
is a well-defined coordinate chart on
\mathcal{M}_{Q,M}
. Now define
u:\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{M,Q}\to\mathbb{R}
, with
\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{M,Q}
the interior of the manifold with boundary
\mathcal{M}_{M,Q}
, as follows:

u=v-2r_{*}.

Since
r_{*}(r_{\sharp})=0
, we have that
u(\tau,r_{\sharp},\theta,\varphi)=v(\tau,r_{\sharp},\theta,\varphi)=\tau+\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{\sharp}}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}(r^{\prime})\,dr^{\prime}
and

u(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)=\tau+\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{\sharp}}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}(r^{\prime})\,dr^{\prime}+\int_{r_{\sharp}}^{r}\left[\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}(r^{\prime})-2\Omega^{-2}\right](r^{\prime})\,dr^{\prime}=\tau+\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{\sharp}}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}(r^{\prime})\,dr^{\prime}-\int_{r_{\sharp}}^{r}\Omega^{-2}\mathbbm{h}(r^{\prime})\,dr^{\prime}.

We conclude that
\lim_{r\to\infty}u(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)
is well-defined if
\Omega^{-2}\mathbbm{h}
is integrable in
[r_{0},\infty)
, with
r_{0}>r_{+}
arbitrary. We will therefore assume that
h=O(r^{-1-\delta})
for some
\delta>0
. For the sake of convenience, we consider the also the following further restrictions: let
r_{I}>r_{\sharp}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbbm{h}(r)=

\displaystyle\>0,\quad

\displaystyle r\geq r_{I}>r_{\sharp},

(2.1)

\displaystyle\Omega^{-2}\mathbbm{h}(r)=

\displaystyle\>h_{0}r^{-2}+O_{\infty}(r^{-3}),\quad

\displaystyle h_{0}>0.

(2.2)

It will also be convenient to consider: let
r_{+}<r_{H}<r_{\sharp}
, then

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}(r)=

\displaystyle\>0,\quad

\displaystyle r\leq r_{H}<r_{\sharp},

(2.3)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}(r)>

\displaystyle\>0,\quad

\displaystyle r_{+}\leq r\leq r_{\sharp}

(2.4)

If (
2.1
) holds, then
\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r>r_{I}\}
are null hypersurfaces. Similarly, if (
2.3
) holds, then
\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r<r_{H}\}
are null hypersurfaces. If (
2.2
) and (
2.4
) hold, then
\Sigma_{\tau}
are globally spacelike and
asymptotically hyperboloidal
.

Then
g_{M,Q}
attains the following form with respect to
(u,\rho_{\infty},\theta,\varphi)
coordinates:

g_{M,Q}=r^{2}(\Omega^{2}\rho_{\infty}^{2}du^{2}+2dud\rho_{\infty}+\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}).

We moreover have that:
\mathcal{M}_{M,Q}\cong\mathcal{H}^{+}\cup\left(\mathbb{R}_{u}\times(0,r_{+}^{-1})_{\rho_{\infty}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\right)
. Then we extend
\mathcal{M}_{M,Q}
by defining:

\widehat{\mathcal{M}_{M,Q}}:=\mathcal{H}^{+}\cup(\mathbb{R}_{u}\times[0,r_{+}^{-1})_{\rho_{\infty}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}).

We denote the level set
\{\rho_{\infty}=0\}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{M}_{M,Q}}
as follows:

\mathcal{I}^{+}:=\{\rho_{\infty}=0\}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{M}_{M,Q}}.

We refer to
\mathcal{I}^{+}
as
future null infinity
.

Note that with the choices (
2.1
) or (
2.2
),
(\tau,\rho_{\infty},\theta,\varphi)
cover the extended manifold
\widehat{\mathcal{M}_{M,Q}}
(away from half great circles on the spheres of constant
\rho_{\infty},\tau
, which are not covered by the chart
(\theta,\varphi)
).

We moreover denote with
S^{2}_{\tau^{\prime},r^{\prime}}
,
S^{2}_{v^{\prime},r^{\prime}}
and
S^{2}_{v^{\prime},r^{\prime}}
the 2-spheres that form the intersection of the level set
\{r=r^{\prime}\}
with the level sets
\{\tau=\tau^{\prime}\}
,
\{v=v^{\prime}\}
, or
\{u=u^{\prime}\}
.

When
\kappa_{+}=0
, the map
(v,\rho_{+},\theta,\varphi)\mapsto(u,\rho_{\infty},\theta,\varphi)
is a
conformal transformation
of
(\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{M,Q},g_{M,Q})
, which is called a
Couch–Torrence transformation
.

Define:

s_{\infty}=r-r_{+}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad s_{+}=r_{+}^{2}(r-r_{+})^{-1}=r_{+}^{2}s_{\infty}^{-1}.

Note that
s_{+},s_{\infty}\in(0,\infty)
. Then the Couch–Torrence transformation can then alternatively be identified with the map:
(v,s_{+},\theta,\varphi)\mapsto(u,r_{+}^{2}s_{+}^{-1},\theta,\varphi)
.

In slight abuse of notation, we will denote in the paragraph below by
\mathbbm{h},\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}},r,\Omega^{2}:(0,\infty)_{s_{+}}\to[0,\infty)
the composition functions defined as follows:
\mathbbm{h}(s_{+})=\mathbbm{h}(r(s_{+}))
,
\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}(s_{+})=\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}(r(s_{+}))
,
r(s_{+})=r_{+}^{2}s_{+}^{-1}+r_{+}
and
\Omega^{2}(s_{+})=\Omega^{2}(r(s_{+}))=(r_{+}^{-1}s_{+}+1)^{-2}
. In the case
|Q|=M
, it will be convenient to introduce the functions
\mathbbm{h},\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}^{+}:(0,\infty)\to[0,1]
, defined as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}^{+}(s_{\infty}):=

\displaystyle\>(r^{2}\mathbbm{h})(r_{+}^{2}s_{\infty}^{-1}),

(2.5)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{h}^{+}(s_{\infty}):=

\displaystyle\>(\Omega^{2}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}})(r_{+}^{2}s_{\infty}^{-1})=2-\Omega^{2}(r_{+}^{2}s_{\infty}^{-1})\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}^{+}(s_{\infty}).

(2.6)

Note that

When integrating over the unit round sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, we will make use of the notation:

d\sigma:=\sin\theta d\theta d\varphi.

2.3.
Key vector fields

The following vector fields will play key roles in the analysis in the remainder of the article. With respect to
(v,r,\theta,\varphi)
coordinates:

\displaystyle T:=

\displaystyle\>\partial_{v},

\displaystyle X:=

\displaystyle\>\partial_{r}+\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}\partial_{v},

\displaystyle\underline{L}:=

\displaystyle\>-\frac{\Omega^{2}}{2}\partial_{r},

\displaystyle L:=

\displaystyle\>T-\underline{L},

\displaystyle Y_{*}:=

\displaystyle L-\underline{L}=\Omega^{2}X+(1-\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}\Omega^{2})T.

With respect to
(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)
coordinates, we have that
T=\partial_{\tau}
and
X=\partial_{r}
.

We will also denote for
q\in\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle K:=

\displaystyle\>T+iqr_{+}^{-1}\mathbf{1},

where we will define
q
according to (
2.8
) below.

2.4.
Charged scalar field equation

The inhomogeneous charged scalar field equation for
\phi:\mathcal{M}_{M,Q}\to\mathbb{C}
on
(\mathcal{M}_{M,Q},g_{M,Q})
is:

(g^{-1}_{M,Q})^{\mu\nu}(^{A}D_{\mu})(^{A}D_{\nu})\phi=G_{A},

(2.7)

where
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{M}_{M,Q})
satisfies
dA=F_{Q}
and is called an
electromagnetic gauge
, with
F_{Q}\in\Omega^{2}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{M,Q})
the Reissner–Nordström
Faraday tensor
, which is defined as follows:
F_{Q}:=-\frac{Q}{r^{2}}dv\wedge dr
, with
Q\in\mathbb{R}
the total
electric charge
in the Reissner–Nordström black hole exterior. Furthermore,

{}^{A}D:=\nabla-i\mathfrak{q}A\otimes(\cdot),

is the
electromagnetic gauge derivative
, with
\nabla
the Levi-Civita covariant derivative corresponding to
g_{M,Q}
with
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{R}
the
scalar field charge parameter
or
charge coupling constant
. We introduce the following (dimensionless) charge parameter:

q:=\mathfrak{q}Q.

(2.8)

In (
2.7
), we applied the following notational convention:

\displaystyle(^{A}D_{\mu})(^{A}D_{\nu})T:=

\displaystyle\>((^{A}D)^{2}T)_{\mu\nu}\quad\textnormal{if $T$ is a tensor field}.

It will moreover be convenient to denote:

{}^{A}D_{Y}:=\nabla_{Y}-i\mathfrak{q}A(Y)\mathbf{1}\quad\textnormal{for any vector field $Y$}.

While we are mainly interested in (
2.7
) with
G_{A}\equiv 0
, we will consider also non-vanishing
G_{A}
in part of the analysis.

In the remainder of the article, it will be more convenient to work with the quantity

\psi:=r\cdot\phi.

Then (
2.7
) is equivalent to:

r(g^{-1}_{M,Q})^{\mu\nu}(^{A}D_{\mu})(^{A}D_{\nu})(r^{-1}\psi)=rG_{A}.

(2.9)

Note that we can alternatively express
F_{Q}:=-\frac{Q}{r^{2}}du\wedge dr=Qdu\wedge dx
in
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{M,Q}\setminus\mathcal{H}^{+}
, so
F_{Q}\in\Omega^{2}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{M,Q})
. We can choose
A\in\Omega^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{M,Q})
, for example, we can consider (
2.1
) or (
2.2
) and take
A=\widehat{A}
with:

\widehat{A}:=-\frac{Q}{r}d\tau.

In most of the analysis in the present paper, we will restrict to
A=\widehat{A}
.

In order to state the integrated energy estimates below, it will also be convenient to consider
A\notin\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{M}_{M,Q})
but
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{M,Q})
. For example, we can consider
A=\widetilde{A}
with

\widetilde{A}:=-\frac{Q}{r}dt.

In that case, (
2.7
) is only valid in
\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{M,Q}
.

Suppose that
A^{\prime}=A+df
for some
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{M,Q})
and
\phi^{\prime}=e^{i\mathfrak{q}f}\phi
. Then for any vector field
Y
, we have that:

{}^{A^{\prime}}D\phi^{\prime}=e^{i\mathfrak{q}f}(^{A}D\phi).

Hence
\phi
is a solution to (
2.7
) if and only if
\phi^{\prime}
satisfies:

(g^{-1}_{M,Q})^{\mu\nu}(^{A^{\prime}}D)_{\mu}(^{A^{\prime}}D)_{\nu}\phi^{\prime}=e^{i\mathfrak{q}f}G_{A}=:G_{A^{\prime}}.

We refer to
\phi^{\prime}
and
G_{A^{\prime}}
as
gauge transformations
of
\phi
and
G_{A}
respectively. In particular, the norms
|\phi|
and
|G_{A}|
are gauge invariant.

We can relate the electromagnetic gauge choices
\widehat{A}
and
\widetilde{A}
as follows:

\widetilde{A}=-\frac{Q}{r}d(v-r_{*})=-\frac{Q}{r}(d\tau-\Omega^{-2}dr+\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}dr)=\widehat{A}+d\left(\int_{r_{\sharp}}^{r}\frac{Q}{r^{\prime}}(\Omega^{-2}-\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}})(r^{\prime})\,dr^{\prime}\right).

An important role will be played by (
2.7
) with
g_{M,Q}
replaced by the Minkowski metric
m=g_{0,0}
and
G_{A}\equiv 0
, but
A
as in (
2.7
):

(m^{-1})^{\mu\nu}(^{A}D_{\mu})(^{A}D_{\nu})(r^{-1}\psi)=0.

(2.10)

In other words, we consider the limits
M\downarrow 0
and
Q\to 0
in (
2.7
), but keep
\mathfrak{q}Q
constant.

The following proposition provides more explicit expressions for (
2.7
) with respect to
\widehat{A}
in terms
\psi
:

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
\psi
be a solution to (
2.9
) with
A=\widehat{A}
. Then:

rG_{\widehat{A}}=X(\Omega^{2}X\psi)+r^{-2}\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\psi-\mathbbm{h}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}T^{2}\psi-2(1-\mathbbm{h})(T+iqr^{-1})X\psi\\
+\left(\frac{d\mathbbm{h}}{dr}-2iqr^{-1}\mathbbm{h}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}\right)T\psi-\left[\frac{d\Omega^{2}}{dr}r^{-1}-iqr^{-1}\frac{d\mathbbm{h}}{dr}-q^{2}\mathbbm{h}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}r^{-2}-iqr^{-2}(1-\mathbbm{h})\right]\psi.

(2.11)

With respect to the coordinate chart
(\tau,\rho_{\infty},\theta,\varphi)
we moreover obtain:

r^{2}(rG_{\widehat{A}})=\partial_{\rho_{\infty}}(\Omega^{2}r^{-2}\partial_{\rho_{\infty}}\psi)+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\psi-\rho_{\infty}^{-2}\mathbbm{h}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}T^{2}\psi+2(1-\mathbbm{h})\partial_{\rho_{\infty}}T\psi+2iq(1-\mathbbm{h})\rho_{\infty}\partial_{\rho_{\infty}}\psi\\
+\left(-\frac{d\mathbbm{h}}{d\rho_{\infty}}-2iq\rho_{\infty}^{-1}\mathbbm{h}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}\right)T\psi-\left[r\frac{d\Omega^{2}}{dr}+iq\rho_{\infty}\frac{d\mathbbm{h}}{d\rho_{\infty}}-q^{2}\mathbbm{h}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}-iq(1-\mathbbm{h})\right]\psi

(2.12)

and with respect to the coordinate chart
(\tau,\rho_{+},\theta,\varphi)
, we obtain:

r^{2}(rG_{\widehat{A}})=\partial_{\rho_{+}}(\Omega^{2}r^{-2}\partial_{\rho_{+}}\psi)+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\psi-r^{2}\mathbbm{h}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}K^{2}\psi-2(1-\mathbbm{h})\partial_{\rho_{+}}K\psi+2iq(1-\mathbbm{h})\rho_{+}\partial_{\rho_{+}}\psi\\
+\left(r^{2}\frac{d\mathbbm{h}}{dr}+2iq\rho_{+}r^{2}\mathbbm{h}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}\right)K\psi-\left[r\frac{d\Omega^{2}}{dr}+iq\rho_{+}r^{2}\frac{d\mathbbm{h}}{dr}-q^{2}\rho_{+}^{2}r^{2}\mathbbm{h}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}-iq(1-\mathbbm{h})\right]\psi.

(2.13)

In particular, when
\kappa_{+}=0
, then

r^{2}(rG_{\widehat{A}})=\partial_{\rho_{\infty}}(\Omega^{2}r^{-2}\partial_{\rho_{\infty}}\psi)+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\psi-\rho_{\infty}^{-2}\mathbbm{h}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}T^{2}\psi+2(1-\mathbbm{h})\partial_{\rho_{\infty}}T\psi+2iq(1-\mathbbm{h})\rho_{\infty}\partial_{\rho_{\infty}}\psi\\
+\left(-\frac{d\mathbbm{h}}{d\rho_{\infty}}-2iq\rho_{\infty}^{-1}\mathbbm{h}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}\right)T\psi-\left[2\rho_{+}\rho_{\infty}+iq\rho_{\infty}\frac{d\mathbbm{h}}{d\rho_{\infty}}-q^{2}\mathbbm{h}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}-iq(1-\mathbbm{h})\right]\psi\\
=\partial_{\rho_{+}}(\Omega^{2}r^{-2}\partial_{\rho_{+}}\psi)+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\psi-\rho_{+}^{-2}\mathbbm{h}^{+}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}^{+}K^{2}\psi+2(1-\mathbbm{h}^{+})\partial_{\rho_{+}}K\psi+2i(-q)(1-\mathbbm{h}_{+})\rho_{+}\partial_{\rho_{+}}\psi\\
+\left(-\frac{d\mathbbm{h}^{+}}{d\rho_{+}}-2i(-q)\rho_{+}^{-1}\mathbbm{h}^{+}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}^{+}\right)K\psi-\left[2\rho_{\infty}\rho_{+}+i(-q)\rho_{+}\frac{d\mathbbm{h}^{+}}{d\rho_{+}}-(-q)^{2}\mathbbm{h}^{+}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}^{+}-i(-q)(1-\mathbbm{h}^{+})\right]\psi.

(2.14)

Note the symmetry between (
2.14
) and (
2.12
): we can obtain the expressions on the very RHS of (
2.14
) from the expressions to the right of the first equality in (
2.12
) by interchanging
\rho_{+}
and
\rho_{\infty}
, replacing
q
with
-q
,
T
with
K
,
\mathbbm{h}
with
\mathbbm{h}_{+}
and
\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}
with
\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}_{+}
.

2.5.
Spherical harmonics

Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
. Then we can decompose:

\displaystyle f=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z},|m|\leq\ell}f_{\ell m}Y_{\ell m},

with
f_{\ell m}\in\mathbb{C}
and
Y_{\ell m}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
spherical harmonics, which form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
, satisfying
\partial_{\varphi}Y_{\ell m}=imY_{\ell m}
and
\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}Y_{\ell m}=-\ell(\ell+1)Y_{\ell m}
. We also denote:

\displaystyle f_{\ell}=

\displaystyle\>\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z},|m|\leq\ell}f_{\ell m}Y_{\ell m},

\displaystyle f_{\geq\ell}=

\displaystyle\>\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell}^{\infty}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z},|m|\leq\ell^{\prime}}f_{\ell^{\prime}m}Y_{\ell^{\prime}m}.

We also apply the above notation to functions on
\mathcal{M}_{M,Q}
or
\Sigma_{\tau}
.

The following parameter plays fundamental role in the present paper when considering
\psi_{\geq\ell}
, with
\psi
a solution to (
2.7
):

\beta_{\ell}:=\sqrt{4\ell(\ell+1)+1-4q^{2}}=\sqrt{(2\ell+1)^{2}-4q^{2}}.

Lemma 2.3
.

(i)

(Poincaré inequality on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
)
Let
f\in H^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
, then:

||f_{\geq\ell}||_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\ell(\ell+1)}}||f||_{H^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}.

(2.15)

(ii)

(Sobolev inequality on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
)
Let
f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
. Then there exists a numerical constant
C>0
, such that:

||f||_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\leq C||f||_{H^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\leq C\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}(\ell+1)^{2}||f_{\ell}||_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}.

(2.16)

Proof.

A straightforward consequence of the expansion
f=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}f_{\ell}
.
∎

2.6.
Commutator vector fields

Let
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}
. We introduce the following shorthand notations for differential operators acting on functions:

\displaystyle D_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\gamma}:=

\displaystyle(^{A}D_{\mathbb{S}^{2}})_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{\gamma_{1}}((r-M)(^{A}D)_{X})^{\gamma_{2}}(^{A}D_{T})^{\gamma_{3}},

(2.17)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}^{\gamma}:=

\displaystyle\>\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{\gamma_{1}}((r-M)X)^{\gamma_{2}}T^{\gamma_{3}}.

(2.18)

with
\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
the Levi-Civita covariant derivative with respect to
\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
and with
D_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}=\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}-i\mathfrak{q}\not{A}
, where
\not{A}\in\Omega^{1}(S^{2}_{\tau,r})
satisfies
\not{A}=A_{\theta}d\theta+A_{\varphi}d\varphi
.

In particular, when
A=\widehat{A}
, we have that:

D_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\gamma}=\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{\gamma_{1}}((r-M)X)^{\gamma_{2}}D_{T}^{\gamma_{3}}.

Given a vector field
Y
and a function
f
, we moreover apply the following notational conventions: for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle D_{Y}D_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\gamma}f:=

\displaystyle\>(^{A}D_{\mathbb{S}^{2}})_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{\gamma_{1}}(^{A}D)_{Y}((r-M)(^{A}D)_{X})^{\gamma_{2}}(^{A}D_{T})^{\gamma_{3}}f,

\displaystyle D_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{k}D_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\gamma}f:=

\displaystyle\>(^{A}D_{\mathbb{S}^{2}})_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{\gamma_{1}+k}((r-M)(^{A}D)_{X})^{\gamma_{2}}(^{A}D_{T})^{\gamma_{3}}f,

\displaystyle D_{Y}\mathbf{Z}^{\gamma}f:=

\displaystyle\>\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{\gamma_{1}}D_{Y}((r-M)X)^{\gamma_{2}}T^{\gamma_{3}}f,

\displaystyle Y\mathbf{Z}^{\gamma}f:=

\displaystyle\>\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{\gamma_{1}}Y((r-M)X)^{\gamma_{2}}T^{\gamma_{3}}f,

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{k}\mathbf{Z}^{\gamma}f:=

\displaystyle\>\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{\gamma_{1}+k}Y((r-M)X)^{\gamma_{2}}T^{\gamma_{3}}f.

2.7.
Energy boundedness and integrated energy decay

We first state a local existence and uniqueness result and then a global integrated energy estimate, coming from
[
Gaj26
]
.

Theorem 2.4
.

(Existence and uniqueness for the charged scalar field equation;
[
Gaj26
]
[Theorem 2.5])

Let
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{M}_{M,Q})
and
G_{A}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{M,Q})
. Consider the initial data pair:

(\upphi,T\upphi)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Sigma_{0})\times C_{c}^{\infty}(\Sigma_{0}).

Then there exists a unique solution
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{M,Q})
